# Gatelab QA Defect Scorecard & Implementation Instructions

## QA Defect Scorecard

| ID | Severity | Area / File | Issue Summary |
|---|---|---|---|
| LAB-01 | Critical | breadboard-view.tsx vs parts.ts | Pin name mismatch ("Q" / "CLK" vs "Y") silently drops manual switch/clock wires from simulation and hides preset switch wires on the breadboard |
| LAB-02 | Critical | circuit-store.ts, place.ts | Hardcoded 14-pin corner power assumptions (Pin 14 / Pin 7) short signal pins (1K, 2CLR) to rails on 16-pin 74LS76A, 74174, and 74175 chips |
| LAB-03 | High | place.ts, breadboard-view.tsx | Adding a Switch, Clock, or LED on the breadboard secretly shorts it to a breadboard strip via seatOnBoard (boardRow: "A" \| "J") even before any wire is drawn |
| LAB-04 | High | breadboard-view.tsx, circuit-store.ts | Dragging an IC horizontally along the breadboard ravine moves the chip (pos.x) but leaves all its hole-to-hole jumpers behind on the old columns |
| LAB-05 | High | place.ts, lab/page.tsx | Switching presets to Breadboard triggers a false ⚠️ 4 parts did not fit on the board toast (counting unmapped schematic power wires as unplaced parts) and drops discrete gate nodes |
| LAB-06 | High | router.ts (buildOccupancy) | Schematic A* router punches pin openings at unrotated coordinates (node.pos + spec.offset), sealing rotated/flipped pins inside obstacle blocks |
| LAB-07 | Medium | presets.ts (mux4) | The 4-to-1 Multiplexer preset is truncated to 2 data inputs (D0, D1) and omits D2 and D3 |
| LAB-08 | Medium | solver.ts (collapseOscillation) | Oscillation collapse omits flopDrivers, causing flip-flop outputs to drop to Z whenever any combinational loop oscillates |
| LAB-09 | Low | gate-symbol.tsx | OR, NOR, XOR, and XNOR symbols lack input lead stubs at x = 0, leaving input pins floating off the curved back |
| XBR-01 | High | use-viewbox.ts, breadboard-view.tsx | No pinch-to-zoom on touch screens, and Breadboard panning requires middle-click or Shift+Drag, making mobile/tablet and trackpad panning impossible |
| XBR-02 | Medium | breadboard-view.tsx (Jumper) | Dragging a jumper's horizontal routing channel mixes raw screen clientY pixels with SVG viewBox units |
| XBR-03 | Medium | breadboard-view.tsx | CSS animate-spin and hover:scale-125 on SVG <circle> elements lack transform-box: fill-box, causing elements to orbit the SVG origin in Safari and Firefox |
| XBR-04 | Medium | breadboard-view.tsx | Fixed trainer chassis width (CHASSIS_W) clips LEDs (>8) and switches (>16) on wider circuits like the 8-bit adder |
| UX-01 | Medium | lab/page.tsx | Clicking the toolbar Trash button (clear()) wipes the entire circuit immediately with no confirmation dialog and does not push to the past undo stack |
| UX-02 | Medium | lab/page.tsx, manual/page.tsx | Right-hand Faults & Verify inspector defaults to closed (rightSidebarOpen = false), hiding live hardware errors, while the Manual lists outdated wire-click shortcuts |

---

## 1. Lab & Circuit Simulator Bugs (And Exact Code Fixes)

### Fix LAB-01 (Trainer Switch & Clock Pin Name Mismatch)
In `src/lib/simulation/parts.ts` (lines 97–109) and `src/lib/simulation/elaborate.ts` (lines 147–161), both switch and clock nodes expose a single output pin named `"Y"`. However, `src/components/lab/breadboard-view.tsx` registers switches as `{ node: sw.id, pin: "Q" }` and clocks as `{ node: clockNode.id, pin: "CLK" }` (lines 146–164, 342, 424, 470, 1219–1223).
- **Why it breaks:** When a user wires a Trainer Switch or Clock to a breadboard hole manually, `buildNetIndex` (`src/lib/simulation/netlist.ts`, line 258) checks `specOf.has(key)`, fails to find `sw::Q` or `clk::CLK`, and silently ignores the wire—so the wire draws on screen while the circuit stays floating (Z). Conversely, when `placeOnBreadboard` seats a preset or synthesized circuit, it creates wires to `pin: "Y"`, which `trainerPinMap` cannot resolve—causing all input switch wires to disappear visually on the breadboard.
- **Code Fix (`src/components/lab/breadboard-view.tsx`):** Change every switch and clock pin reference in `trainerPinMap`, `trainerSocketsList`, `handleClockClick`, `TrainerSwitch`, and `resolveEndpointPoint` from `"Q"` and `"CLK"` to `"Y"`:
```ts
// In trainerPinMap & trainerSocketsList (breadboard-view.tsx):
if (clockNode) register({ node: clockNode.id, pin: "Y" }, { x: 850, y: 55 });
switchNodes.forEach((sw, i) => {
  register({ node: sw.id, pin: "Y" }, { x: 105 + i * 82, y: 668 });
});
```

### Fix LAB-02 (Hardcoded 14-Pin Corner Power Shorting 74LS76A & 16-Pin ICs)
Both `autoWireIcPower` (`src/stores/circuit-store.ts`, lines 464–489) and `placeOnBreadboard` (`src/lib/simulation/place.ts`, lines 167–186) assume VCC is always top-left (col, row F/J) and GND is always bottom-right (col + span - 1, row E/A). On 74LS76A (16-pin DIP with VCC on pin 5 and GND on pin 13), clicking Auto-Power wires Pin 16 (1K) to +5V and Pin 8 (2CLR) to GND, holding flip-flop 2 in permanent reset while leaving the chip unpowered.
- **Code Fix (`src/stores/circuit-store.ts` & `src/lib/simulation/place.ts`):** Resolve the actual VCC and GND holes from `dipHoles` and `def.pins`:
```ts
const holes = dipHoles({ col }, def.pins.length);
const vccIdx = def.pins.findIndex((p) => p.dir === "pwr");
const gndIdx = def.pins.findIndex((p) => p.dir === "gnd");
const vccLeg = holes[vccIdx];
const gndLeg = holes[gndIdx];
// On upper half (row F), free hole is row J -> +top rail; on lower half (row E), row A -> -bottom rail
const vccHole: HoleRef = { col: vccLeg.col, row: vccLeg.row === "F" ? "J" : "A" };
const vccRail: HoleRef = { col: vccLeg.col, row: vccLeg.row === "F" ? "+top" : "+bottom" };
const gndHole: HoleRef = { col: gndLeg.col, row: gndLeg.row === "E" ? "A" : "J" };
const gndRail: HoleRef = { col: gndLeg.col, row: gndLeg.row === "E" ? "-bottom" : "-top" };
```

### Fix LAB-03 (Phantom Shorts When Adding Switches/LEDs on the Breadboard)
When on the breadboard, clicking + Input or + LED (or adding from the Palette) calls `seatOnBoard` (`src/lib/simulation/place.ts`, lines 307–321), which assigns `boardRow: "A"` or `"J"` and `pos: { x: col, y: 0 }`. `seatOf` in `netlist.ts` (lines 354–360) then unions that switch/LED with breadboard strip `col/A` or `col/J`—meaning your Trainer Panel switch is invisibly shorted to column `col` on the breadboard with no jumper wire shown.
- **Code Fix (`src/lib/simulation/place.ts`):** In `seatOnBoard`, only seat `node.kind === "ic"` onto breadboard columns. For `switch`, `clock`, `led`, and `rail`, return `{ ...node, pos: { x: 0, y: 0 }, boardRow: undefined }` so they reside exclusively in the Trainer Panel until wired.

### Fix LAB-04 (Dragging a Breadboard IC Disconnects Its Jumpers)
In BreadboardView (`src/components/lab/breadboard-view.tsx`, line 521), dragging an IC calls `moveNode(node.id, { x: newCol, y: 0 })`. Because breadboard jumpers are stored with HoleRef endpoints (`{ col, row }`), moving the IC leaves its jumpers plugged into the old columns.
- **Code Fix (`src/stores/circuit-store.ts`):** When `doc.board` is active and `moveNode` moves an `ic` from `oldCol` to `newCol` (`delta = newCol - oldCol`), shift any hole wire endpoint whose `col` falls within `[oldCol, oldCol + span - 1]` on terminal rows `A..J` by `delta`. Also disable pointer-events on the `IcXRay` overlay so internal gate SVGs don't block pointer capture when grabbing the chip body.

### Fix LAB-05 (False "4 parts did not fit" Warning & Unmapped Ideal Gates)
In `buildPreset` (`src/lib/simulation/presets.ts`), multi-output presets create multiple rail nodes (`half-adder_S_s4`, `half-adder_C_s4`, etc.). In `placeOnBreadboard` (`panelMode: true`, `src/lib/simulation/place.ts`, lines 189–238), rail nodes are not added to `holeOfPin` because `panelMode` auto-wires IC power. When the loop encounters the schematic's rail -> VCC/GND wires, `endpointHole` returns `null` and pushes those wires into `unplaced`, triggering the misleading toast ⚠️ 4 parts did not fit on the board on Gatelab Lab. Additionally, if the schematic contains discrete gate primitives (such as from the Palette or hierarchical composite presets), `placeOnBreadboard` silently drops them.
- **Code Fix (`src/lib/simulation/place.ts`):** Skip schematic wires connected to rail nodes or IC power pins (VCC/GND) in `panelMode` instead of pushing them to `unplaced`, and technology-map discrete gate nodes into 74xx ICs before seating (or alert specifically when discrete gates are present).

### Fix LAB-06 (Rotated/Flipped Schematic Pins Blocked by Router Occupancy Grid)
In `src/lib/simulation/router.ts` (`buildOccupancy`, line 132), pin cells are unblocked using `{ x: node.pos.x + spec.offset.x, y: node.pos.y + spec.offset.y }`, which ignores `node.rotation`, `node.flipX`, and `node.flipY`. Rotating any gate (R) leaves its actual pins inside -1 obstacle cells, causing A* routing to fail and draw diagonal air-wires.
- **Code Fix (`src/lib/simulation/router.ts`):** Replace line 132 with `const p = getPinWorldPosition(node, spec.name);`.

### Fix LAB-07 (Broken 4-to-1 Mux Preset)
In `src/lib/simulation/presets.ts` (lines 58–64), `mux4` only defines `variables: ["S1", "S0", "D0", "D1"]` and `expr: "S1'*S0'*D0 + S1'*S0*D1"`.
- **Code Fix (`src/lib/simulation/presets.ts`):** Add `"D2", "D3"` to `variables` and update `expr` to `"S1'*S0'*D0 + S1'*S0*D1 + S1*S0'*D2 + S1*S0*D3"`.

### Fix LAB-08 (Oscillation Collapse Drops Flip-Flop Outputs)
In `src/lib/simulation/solver.ts` (`collapseOscillation`, lines 354–361), the local `resolve()` helper omits `flopDrivers` and `flopOut`, resetting flip-flop nets to Z during combinational oscillation. Pass `flopDrivers` and `flopOut` into `collapseOscillation`.

### Fix LAB-09 (Floating Input Pins on Curved Gate Symbols)
In `src/components/lab/gate-symbol.tsx`, add short horizontal input lead lines from `x = 0` to the curved back path of OR, NOR, XOR, and XNOR gates so wires visually meet the symbol at all arities (2, 3, and 4 inputs).

---

## 2. Cross-Browser & Cross-Platform Compatibility Fixes

- **Enable Touch Pan/Pinch-Zoom & Primary-Drag Panning (`use-viewbox.ts` & `breadboard-view.tsx`):**
  - Fix left-click / single touch panning on empty chassis/board areas and touch pinch zoom in `useViewBox`.
- **Fix SVG Transform Origin & Drop-Shadow Glitches on Safari/Firefox (`breadboard-view.tsx`):**
  - Add `[transform-box:fill-box] origin-center` to all transformed SVG child elements and move `drop-shadow(...)` into `style={{ filter: ... }}` or an `<svg><defs><filter>` block.
- **Fix Breadboard Wire Channel Drag Scaling (`breadboard-view.tsx`):**
  - Pass `cam.toView` down to `Jumper` and set `onChannelDrag(Math.round(cam.toView(ev.clientX, ev.clientY).y / 2) * 2)`.
- **Make Trainer Chassis Width Dynamic for Wide Presets (`breadboard-view.tsx`):**
  - Compute `CHASSIS_W = Math.max(BOARD_OFFSET_X * 2 + w, 945 + (ledNodes.length + 1) * 66 + 40, 105 + (switchNodes.length + 1) * 82 + 80)`.

---

## 3. UI/UX Bugs & Workflow Friction

- **Protect the Clear/Trash Action & Support Undo (`circuit-store.ts`, `lab/page.tsx`):**
  - Push previous doc onto `past` inside `clear()` (so Ctrl+Z recovers a cleared board) and add a confirmation or toast action with Undo.
- **Auto-Open or Highlight the Faults/Verify Drawer (`lab/page.tsx`):**
  - Default `rightSidebarOpen` to true on viewports >= 1280px (`xl`), or auto-open it when `expected` is set from Theory.
- **Reconcile Header Numbering & Manual Documentation (`site-header.tsx`, `manual/page.tsx`):**
  - Separate Manual visually with a vertical divider or move it beside Feedback.
  - In Manual and Lab hint bar, change "click a wire to delete it" to "Alt+Click or Delete/Backspace to delete it".

## 4. UI Polish & Engineering Aesthetic

- Reduce border radius and use crisp technical hairlines.
- Replace Sparkles icon on deterministic synthesis tabs with `Cpu`, `Sigma`, or `Binary`.
- Refine breadboard and IC materials, tactile switches, and diffused LEDs.
- Ensure Geist Mono typography for pinouts, net tables, and status readouts.


=== FILE: src/lib/simulation/sequential.ts ===
import { isDefinite, L0, L1, LX, LZ, type Logic } from "./logic";

/**
 * Flip-flops: the first thing in this simulator that has a yesterday.
 *
 * WHY THIS IS A SEPARATE FILE AND A SEPARATE PASS
 * ----------------------------------------------
 * Everything else here is combinational. `solver.ts` iterates a delta-cycle
 * fixpoint until the net vector stops changing, and its whole correctness
 * argument rests on that fixpoint being a pure function of the inputs. A
 * flip-flop breaks that: its output depends on what happened at the last clock
 * edge, and an EDGE is not something a fixpoint can perceive — by the time the
 * loop has settled, the transition that caused it is over.
 *
 * So state is kept out of the fixpoint entirely. Within a settle, a flip-flop is
 * a constant: it drives whatever it is currently storing. Between settles, the
 * solver compares each clock net against its value at the end of the previous
 * settle, and any flip-flop whose clock moved the right way samples its data
 * and updates. Then it settles again, because that output change may have moved
 * another flip-flop's clock — which is exactly what a ripple counter IS, and
 * why the loop has to repeat rather than run once.
 *
 * WHY A FLIP-FLOP POWERS UP AT X. A real one comes up in whichever state the
 * metastability happened to fall into; that is the entire reason every counter
 * you have ever seen has a CLEAR wired to it. Starting at 0 would make a
 * missing reset invisible, which is the same mistake as reading a floating
 * input as false — the one this simulator exists to refuse to make.
 */

export type FlopKind = "d" | "jk" | "t" | "sr";

/** Which clock transition loads the flip-flop. */
export type Edge = "rising" | "falling";

/** The two outputs. They are NOT always complementary — see `asyncOutputs`. */
export interface FlopOutputs {
  readonly q: Logic;
  readonly qn: Logic;
}

/**
 * The asynchronous inputs, both ACTIVE LOW, as every 74xx part has them.
 *
 * `null` means the part has no such pin — a '107 has clear and no preset — and
 * that is different from a pin sitting high. A missing pin can never assert; an
 * unconnected one is floating, and floating is not "inactive".
 */
export interface AsyncInputs {
  readonly preset: Logic | null;
  readonly clear: Logic | null;
}

/**
 * What preset and clear force, or `null` if neither is asserting.
 *
 * The nonstable case is real and is in the datasheet: hold both low and BOTH
 * outputs go high, which is not a state the flip-flop can keep once either one
 * is released. It is reported rather than smoothed over, because a student who
 * has wired preset and clear together has made a mistake worth naming.
 */
export function asyncOutputs(async: AsyncInputs): FlopOutputs | null {
  const { preset, clear } = async;
  const asserted = (v: Logic | null): boolean | null =>
    v === null ? false : v === L0 ? true : v === L1 ? false : null;

  const p = asserted(preset);
  const c = asserted(clear);

  // A floating or conflicting async pin is not "inactive" — it is a pin whose
  // effect nobody can predict, and the honest output is X.
  if (p === null || c === null) return { q: LX, qn: LX };
  if (p && c) return { q: L1, qn: L1 };
  if (p) return { q: L1, qn: L0 };
  if (c) return { q: L0, qn: L1 };
  return null;
}

/** Is this async combination the nonstable both-low one? */
export const bothAsyncAsserted = (async: AsyncInputs): boolean =>
  async.preset === L0 && async.clear === L0;

/**
 * The stored value after a clock edge.
 *
 * `data` is positional per kind: `[d]`, `[j, k]`, `[t]`, `[s, r]`. An
 * indeterminate data input produces an indeterminate state — clocking in a
 * floating D gives you X, not 0. That is the whole point of four-state logic
 * applied to the one place where the mistake becomes permanent: a combinational
 * gate forgets a bad input the moment it goes away, and a flip-flop does not.
 */
export function clockedNext(
  kind: FlopKind,
  data: readonly Logic[],
  q: Logic,
): Logic {
  switch (kind) {
    case "d": {
      const d = data[0] ?? LZ;
      return isDefinite(d) ? d : LX;
    }
    case "t": {
      const t = data[0] ?? LZ;
      if (!isDefinite(t)) return LX;
      if (t === L0) return q;
      return toggle(q);
    }
    case "jk": {
      const j = data[0] ?? LZ;
      const k = data[1] ?? LZ;
      if (!isDefinite(j) || !isDefinite(k)) return LX;
      if (j === L0 && k === L0) return q;
      if (j === L1 && k === L0) return L1;
      if (j === L0 && k === L1) return L0;
      return toggle(q);
    }
    case "sr": {
      const s = data[0] ?? LZ;
      const r = data[1] ?? LZ;
      if (!isDefinite(s) || !isDefinite(r)) return LX;
      if (s === L1 && r === L1) return LX; // forbidden, and it stays forbidden
      if (s === L1) return L1;
      if (r === L1) return L0;
      return q;
    }
  }
}

/** Hold and toggle both have to carry an unknown forward as unknown. */
const toggle = (q: Logic): Logic => (q === L0 ? L1 : q === L1 ? L0 : LX);

/**
 * Did the clock just do the thing this flip-flop triggers on?
 *
 * Only a definite 0->1 or 1->0 counts. A clock coming out of Z or X has not
 * "risen": nobody knows where it was, so nobody knows whether it crossed the
 * threshold, and inventing an edge there would clock in data on a wire that is
 * not connected to anything.
 */
export function isEdge(edge: Edge, before: Logic, after: Logic): boolean {
  if (!isDefinite(before) || !isDefinite(after)) return false;
  return edge === "rising" ? before === L0 && after === L1 : before === L1 && after === L0;
}

/** The outputs a flip-flop drives while it is simply holding `q`. */
export const heldOutputs = (q: Logic): FlopOutputs => ({
  q,
  qn: q === L0 ? L1 : q === L1 ? L0 : q === LZ ? LZ : LX,
});

/** How many data pins each kind has, and what they are called in order. */
export const DATA_PINS: Readonly<Record<FlopKind, readonly string[]>> = {
  d: ["D"],
  t: ["T"],
  jk: ["J", "K"],
  sr: ["S", "R"],
};

/** The power-up state of a real flip-flop: nobody knows. */
export const UNKNOWN: Logic = LX;

/** Never let `LZ` reach a stored bit — a flip-flop cannot store "floating". */
export const asStored = (v: Logic): Logic => (v === LZ ? LX : v);


=== FILE: src/lib/simulation/sequential-presets.ts ===
import {
  asNodeId,
  asWireId,
  pinEnd,
  type CircuitDocument,
  type CircuitNode,
  type NodeId,
  type Wire,
} from "./netlist";

/**
 * Clocked circuits, built from the real flip-flop packages.
 *
 * WHY THESE ARE HAND-WIRED AND THE COMBINATIONAL PRESETS ARE NOT. Those are
 * expressions run through the synthesizer, which is right for them: a function
 * IS its expression, and generating the gates keeps them honest. A counter is
 * not a function of anything — it is a specific arrangement of specific parts,
 * and the arrangement is the entire content of the lesson. "Stage 0's Q drives
 * stage 1's clock" cannot be written as an expression, and if it could, the
 * thing that made it a ripple counter would have been optimised away.
 *
 * Every one of these is checked by clocking it and reading the count, in
 * `sequential-presets.test.ts`. A miswired preset is not a picture that looks
 * slightly wrong; it is a circuit that counts 0, 1, 0, 2 and teaches that.
 */

export interface SequentialPreset {
  readonly id: string;
  readonly name: string;
  readonly description: string;
  /** The lesson, shown under the name when it is opened. */
  readonly note?: string;
  readonly build: () => CircuitDocument;
}

/** A minimal document builder. Pin-to-pin only; these never touch a board. */
class Board {
  private readonly nodes: Record<string, CircuitNode> = {};
  private readonly wires: Record<string, Wire> = {};
  private n = 0;

  ic(id: string, part: string, x: number, y: number): this {
    this.nodes[id] = { kind: "ic", id: asNodeId(id), label: id, part, pos: { x, y } };
    return this;
  }
  rail(id: string, rail: "vcc" | "gnd", x: number, y: number): this {
    this.nodes[id] = { kind: "rail", id: asNodeId(id), label: id, rail, pos: { x, y } };
    return this;
  }
  switch(id: string, state: 0 | 1, x: number, y: number): this {
    this.nodes[id] = { kind: "switch", id: asNodeId(id), label: id, state, pos: { x, y } };
    return this;
  }
  clock(id: string, x: number, y: number): this {
    this.nodes[id] = { kind: "clock", id: asNodeId(id), label: id, state: 0, pos: { x, y } };
    return this;
  }
  led(id: string, x: number, y: number): this {
    this.nodes[id] = { kind: "led", id: asNodeId(id), label: id, pos: { x, y } };
    return this;
  }
  wire(a: NodeId | string, ap: string, b: NodeId | string, bp: string): this {
    const id = asWireId(`w${(this.n += 1)}`);
    this.wires[id] = {
      id,
      a: pinEnd({ node: asNodeId(String(a)), pin: ap }),
      b: pinEnd({ node: asNodeId(String(b)), pin: bp }),
    };
    return this;
  }
  /** Power a package from the two rails, by its own supply pin names. */
  power(id: string): this {
    return this.wire("V", "VCC", id, "VCC").wire("G", "GND", id, "GND");
  }
  build(): CircuitDocument {
    return { nodes: this.nodes, wires: this.wires };
  }
}

const rails = (b: Board): Board => b.rail("V", "vcc", 20, 20).rail("G", "gnd", 100, 20);

/**
 * An n-bit RIPPLE counter: every stage clocked by the stage below it.
 *
 * Two flip-flops per '107 package. Each is in toggle mode (J and K tied high),
 * so it flips on every clock it sees — and what stage i sees is stage i-1's
 * output. That is the whole circuit, and it is also the whole problem with it:
 * the top bit cannot settle until the carry has walked all the way up.
 */
function rippleCounter(bits: number): CircuitDocument {
  const b = rails(new Board());
  b.clock("CLK", 20, 120).switch("CLR", 1, 20, 200);

  const packages = Math.ceil(bits / 2);
  for (let i = 0; i < packages; i++) b.ic(`U${i + 1}`, "74LS107A", 200 + i * 260, 100);
  for (let i = 0; i < packages; i++) b.power(`U${i + 1}`);

  for (let bit = 0; bit < bits; bit++) {
    const chip = `U${Math.floor(bit / 2) + 1}`;
    const slot = (bit % 2) + 1;
    // Toggle mode, and a clear that reaches every stage.
    b.wire("V", "VCC", chip, `${slot}J`);
    b.wire("V", "VCC", chip, `${slot}K`);
    b.wire("CLR", "Y", chip, `${slot}CLR`);

    if (bit === 0) {
      b.wire("CLK", "Y", chip, `${slot}CLK`);
    } else {
      const prev = `U${Math.floor((bit - 1) / 2) + 1}`;
      const prevSlot = ((bit - 1) % 2) + 1;
      b.wire(prev, `${prevSlot}Q`, chip, `${slot}CLK`);
    }

    b.led(`Q${bit}`, 640 + Math.floor(bit / 2) * 60, 80 + (bit % 2) * 80);
    b.wire(chip, `${slot}Q`, `Q${bit}`, "A");
  }
  return b.build();
}

/**
 * A 2-bit SYNCHRONOUS counter, for contrast: one clock, both stages.
 *
 * Stage 1 toggles only when stage 0 is high, which is what its J and K being
 * tied to Q0 says. Both flip-flops see the same edge at the same instant, so
 * there is no ripple and no settling delay — the difference this preset exists
 * to show, sitting next to the ripple one in the menu.
 */
function synchronousCounter(): CircuitDocument {
  const b = rails(new Board());
  b.clock("CLK", 20, 120).switch("CLR", 1, 20, 200).ic("U1", "74LS107A", 200, 100);
  b.power("U1");

  // Stage 0: free-running toggle.
  b.wire("V", "VCC", "U1", "1J").wire("V", "VCC", "U1", "1K");
  // Stage 1: toggles only when stage 0 is already high.
  b.wire("U1", "1Q", "U1", "2J").wire("U1", "1Q", "U1", "2K");
  // One clock to both. THIS is what makes it synchronous.
  b.wire("CLK", "Y", "U1", "1CLK").wire("CLK", "Y", "U1", "2CLK");
  b.wire("CLR", "Y", "U1", "1CLR").wire("CLR", "Y", "U1", "2CLR");

  b.led("Q0", 520, 80).led("Q1", 520, 180);
  b.wire("U1", "1Q", "Q0", "A").wire("U1", "2Q", "Q1", "A");
  return b.build();
}

/** A 4-bit parallel register: four D inputs, one clock, one clear. */
function register(): CircuitDocument {
  const b = rails(new Board());
  b.clock("CLK", 20, 120).switch("CLR", 1, 20, 190).ic("U1", "74175", 260, 90);
  b.power("U1");
  b.wire("CLK", "Y", "U1", "CLK").wire("CLR", "Y", "U1", "CLR");

  for (const i of [1, 2, 3, 4]) {
    b.switch(`D${i}`, 0, 20, 260 + (i - 1) * 60);
    b.led(`Q${i}`, 600, 60 + (i - 1) * 60);
    b.wire(`D${i}`, "Y", "U1", `${i}D`);
    b.wire("U1", `${i}Q`, `Q${i}`, "A");
  }
  return b.build();
}

/**
 * A 4-bit SHIFT register: the same '175, with each Q feeding the next D.
 *
 * The only difference between this and the register above is four wires, which
 * is the point worth seeing — a register that feeds itself sideways is a shift
 * register, and there is no separate part involved.
 */
function shiftRegister(): CircuitDocument {
  const b = rails(new Board());
  b.clock("CLK", 20, 120).switch("CLR", 1, 20, 190).switch("SIN", 0, 20, 260);
  b.ic("U1", "74175", 260, 90);
  b.power("U1");
  b.wire("CLK", "Y", "U1", "CLK").wire("CLR", "Y", "U1", "CLR");
  b.wire("SIN", "Y", "U1", "1D");

  for (const i of [2, 3, 4]) b.wire("U1", `${i - 1}Q`, "U1", `${i}D`);
  for (const i of [1, 2, 3, 4]) {
    b.led(`Q${i}`, 600, 60 + (i - 1) * 60);
    b.wire("U1", `${i}Q`, `Q${i}`, "A");
  }
  return b.build();
}

/**
 * One '74 wired as a divide-by-two: D fed from its own Q-bar.
 *
 * The smallest sequential circuit worth drawing, and the one that shows what
 * "edge-triggered" buys you — the same wiring on a transparent latch would
 * oscillate as fast as the gates allowed rather than divide anything.
 */
function divideByTwo(): CircuitDocument {
  const b = rails(new Board());
  b.clock("CLK", 20, 120).switch("CLR", 1, 20, 200).ic("U1", "7474", 220, 100);
  b.power("U1");
  b.wire("CLK", "Y", "U1", "1CLK");
  b.wire("U1", "1QN", "U1", "1D");
  b.wire("CLR", "Y", "U1", "1CLR");
  b.wire("V", "VCC", "U1", "1PRE");
  b.led("Q", 520, 100);
  b.wire("U1", "1Q", "Q", "A");
  return b.build();
}

export const SEQUENTIAL_PRESETS: readonly SequentialPreset[] = [
  {
    id: "divide-by-two",
    name: "Divide by two",
    description: "One D flip-flop with its own Q̄ fed back to D.",
    note: "The output changes once per clock pulse, so it runs at half the clock rate. Chain these and you have a ripple counter.",
    build: divideByTwo,
  },
  {
    id: "ripple-2",
    name: "2-bit ripple counter",
    description: "Two J-K flip-flops, each clocked by the one below it.",
    build: () => rippleCounter(2),
  },
  {
    id: "ripple-4",
    name: "4-bit ripple counter",
    description: "Four toggling flip-flops in a chain. Counts 0 to 15.",
    note: "Every stage is clocked by the one below it, so the top bit cannot settle until the carry has walked all the way up. Watch the clear: the flip-flops power up unknown.",
    build: () => rippleCounter(4),
  },
  {
    id: "sync-2",
    name: "2-bit synchronous counter",
    description: "Both flip-flops on one clock; stage 1 toggles when stage 0 is high.",
    note: "The same count as the ripple version, with one wire moved. Every stage sees the same edge at the same instant, so there is no settling delay to wait out.",
    build: synchronousCounter,

export const getSequentialPreset = (id: string): SequentialPreset | undefined =>
  SEQUENTIAL_PRESETS.find((p) => p.id === id);


=== FILE: src/lib/simulation/solver.ts ===
import {
  evalGate,
  resolveNet,
  L0,
  L1,
  LX,
  LZ,
  type Driver,
  type Logic,
} from "./logic";
import type { SimFlop, SimNetlist } from "./elaborate";
import {
  asStored,
  asyncOutputs,
  clockedNext,
  heldOutputs,
  isEdge,
  UNKNOWN,
} from "./sequential";

/**
 * Event-driven, double-buffered delta-cycle solver.
 *
 * WHY NOT A TOPOLOGICAL SORT? Because it requires a DAG, and the user can — and
 * in a digital logic lab, WILL — wire an output back to an input. The moment
 * they cross-couple two NAND gates into a latch, a levelized sort has no valid
 * order at all. An event-driven fixpoint handles feedback natively, and as a
 * bonus it makes a switch toggle cost O(activity) instead of O(whole circuit).
 *
 * THE LOAD-BEARING DETAIL: evaluation reads from `cur` and writes to `next`,
 * then swaps. It does NOT iterate in place.
 *
 * Iterate in place and a cross-coupled latch "settles" to whatever the pop order
 * happened to produce — a different, entirely plausible-looking answer depending
 * on which cell you visited first. Results become irreproducible, tests become
 * flaky, and period-2 oscillation vanishes because it gets absorbed into the
 * iteration order.
 *
 * Double-buffering makes the transition `state[k+1] = f(state[k])` a
 * deterministic pure function of the whole net-value vector. Three things fall
 * out of that:
 *   - reproducible results, hence testable ones;
 *   - period-2 oscillation actually manifests as period-2 oscillation;
 *   - and since the state space is finite (4^nets) and f is deterministic, the
 *     sequence is *provably eventually periodic* — so hash-based cycle detection
 *     is complete, not a heuristic. The iteration cap is then a performance
 *     guard, not a correctness crutch.
 */

export interface SimState {
  /** Net ordinal -> Logic. */
  readonly values: Uint8Array;
  readonly settled: boolean;
  readonly deltaCycles: number;
  /** Net ordinals that never stopped changing. Empty when settled. */
  readonly oscillating: readonly number[];
  /** 0 when settled. */
  readonly period: number;
  /** What every flip-flop is storing now. Feed it back in for the next step. */
  readonly state: SeqState;
  /** How many flip-flops loaded during this evaluation. */
  readonly edges: number;
}

/**
 * Everything the circuit remembers between evaluations.
 *
 * `clk` is not an optimisation — it is the only place an EDGE can live. A
 * fixpoint sees levels; by the time it has settled, the transition that caused
 * the settle is over and unrecoverable. Keeping each flip-flop's view of its own
 * clock from the end of the previous evaluation is what lets the next one say
 * "that went from 0 to 1".
 */
export interface SeqState {
  /** Per flip-flop, the stored Q. */
  readonly q: Uint8Array;
  /** Per flip-flop, its clock net's value at the end of the last evaluation. */
  readonly clk: Uint8Array;
}

/**
 * A circuit that has not been clocked yet.
 *
 * Q starts UNKNOWN, not 0. A real flip-flop powers up in whichever state the
 * metastability fell into — which is the entire reason every counter ever built
 * has a clear wired to it. Starting at 0 would make a missing reset invisible,
 * which is the same mistake as reading a floating input as false.
 */
export const initialState = (nl: SimNetlist): SeqState => ({
  q: new Uint8Array(nl.flops.length).fill(UNKNOWN),
  clk: new Uint8Array(nl.flops.length).fill(LZ),
});

export const cloneState = (state: SeqState): SeqState => ({
  q: new Uint8Array(state.q),
  clk: new Uint8Array(state.clk),
});

/**
 * The stored state, keyed by WHICH FLIP-FLOP rather than by array position.
 *
 * The netlist is rebuilt from the document on every simulation, so a flip-flop's
 * index in `nl.flops` is only as stable as the document's node order — add a
 * chip and every index after it shifts. Carrying the state across in an array
 * would then hand flip-flop 3's bit to flip-flop 4, which is a memory circuit
 * silently changing its mind about what it remembered. The identity of a
 * flip-flop is its package and its slot, so that is the key.
 *
 * Plain JSON, so it survives `persist` and a page reload.
 */
export type StoredState = Readonly<Record<string, readonly [Logic, Logic]>>;

const keyOf = (flop: SimFlop): string => `${flop.owner.node}/${flop.owner.slot}`;

export function toSeqState(nl: SimNetlist, stored: StoredState | undefined): SeqState {
  const state = initialState(nl);
  if (!stored) return state;
  for (const flop of nl.flops) {
    const saved = stored[keyOf(flop)];
    if (!saved) continue;
    state.q[flop.id] = saved[0];
    state.clk[flop.id] = saved[1];
  }
  return state;
}

export function fromSeqState(nl: SimNetlist, state: SeqState): StoredState {
  const out: Record<string, readonly [Logic, Logic]> = {};
  for (const flop of nl.flops) {
    out[keyOf(flop)] = [state.q[flop.id] as Logic, state.clk[flop.id] as Logic];
  }
  return out;
}

export interface SimOptions {
  readonly maxDeltaCycles?: number;
  /**
   * The initial value of every CELL OUTPUT — which is what a feedback loop
   * starts from, and therefore the only thing that can steer it to one stable
   * state rather than another.
   *
   * (Seeding the *net* array instead would do nothing at all: the first thing
   * the solver does is resolve every net from its drivers, which overwrites it.
   * That mistake made the two-seed memory check below compare two identical
   * runs.)
   *
   * An acyclic circuit converges to the same fixpoint from ANY seed — its
   * outputs are a function of its inputs, full stop. A circuit with memory does
   * not. So running it twice from `L0` and from `L1` and comparing is a sound
   * test for "this thing has state", and that is exactly what verify() does.
   */
  readonly seed?: Logic;
  /** What the flip-flops were storing. Omit to start from power-up. */
  readonly state?: SeqState;
  /**
   * Override each clock's level, positionally against `netlist.clocks`.
   *
   * Without this, driving a clock would mean editing the document and
   * re-elaborating — fine for a button press, hopeless for a timing sweep that
   * moves it a thousand times.
   */
  readonly clocks?: readonly (0 | 1)[];
}

const DEFAULT_MAX_CYCLES = 256;

/** Input vector: one bit per `netlist.inputs` entry, in that order. */
export type InputVector = readonly (0 | 1)[];

/**
 * PURE. Fresh buffers, no mutation of the netlist, no reference to the document.
 * This is what the M6 verification sweep calls 2^n times.
 *
 * SETTLE, LOOK FOR EDGES, SETTLE AGAIN.
 *
 * With no flip-flops in the circuit this is exactly one settle and nothing
 * else — the combinational path is untouched. With flip-flops it becomes a
 * short outer loop, and the loop is not an implementation detail: it is what a
 * RIPPLE counter is. Stage 0 toggles, and stage 0's own output is stage 1's
 * clock, so the edge that advances stage 1 does not exist until stage 0 has
 * already moved. One pass per stage, in order, is the delay you can see on a
 * real ripple counter's scope trace.
 *
 * Inside any one settle a flip-flop is a CONSTANT — it drives what it is
 * storing — so the fixpoint keeps being a pure function of the net vector and
 * every property that rested on that still holds.
 */
export function evaluate(
  nl: SimNetlist,
  inputs: InputVector,
  options: SimOptions = {},
): SimState {
  const state = cloneState(options.state ?? initialState(nl));
  if (nl.flops.length === 0) {
    return { ...settle(nl, inputs, options, state), state, edges: 0 };
  }

  // One pass per flip-flop is enough for the longest possible ripple, plus one
  // to confirm that nothing moved. More than that is a circuit clocking itself
  // — a ring oscillator — and it is reported rather than looped on forever.
  const maxPasses = nl.flops.length + 2;
  let last = settle(nl, inputs, options, state);
  let edges = 0;

  for (let pass = 0; pass < maxPasses; pass++) {
    const moved = advance(nl, last.values, state);
    edges += moved;
    if (moved === 0) return { ...last, state, edges };
    last = settle(nl, inputs, options, state);
  }

  return { ...last, settled: false, state, edges };
}

/**
 * Apply one round of asynchronous overrides and clock edges to the stored state.
 *
 * Returns how many flip-flops actually loaded, which is the loop's termination
 * condition above.
 */
function advance(nl: SimNetlist, values: Uint8Array, state: SeqState): number {
  let moved = 0;

  for (const flop of nl.flops) {
    const clockNow = values[flop.clock] as Logic;
    const wasClock = state.clk[flop.id] as Logic;
    state.clk[flop.id] = clockNow;

    if (flop.power && !isPowered(flop.power, values)) {
      // An unpowered flip-flop is not holding anything. It has no supply to
      // hold it with, and saying it still remembers the last value would be the
      // same lie as an unpowered gate driving a clean 0.
      state.q[flop.id] = UNKNOWN;
      continue;
    }

    // Preset and clear are LEVEL-sensitive and outrank the clock, so they are
    // applied first and they write the stored bit — otherwise releasing clear
    // would reveal whatever was underneath it.
    const async = asyncOutputs(asyncOf(flop, values));
    if (async) {
      const forced = asStored(async.q);
      if (state.q[flop.id] !== forced) moved += 1;
      state.q[flop.id] = forced;
      continue;
    }

    if (!isEdge(flop.edge, wasClock, clockNow)) continue;

    const before = state.q[flop.id] as Logic;
    const after = asStored(
      clockedNext(flop.kind, flop.data.map((n) => values[n] as Logic), before),
    );
    state.q[flop.id] = after;
    if (after !== before) moved += 1;
  }

  return moved;
}

const asyncOf = (flop: SimFlop, values: Uint8Array) => ({
  preset: flop.preset === null ? null : ((values[flop.preset] ?? LZ) as Logic),
  clear: flop.clear === null ? null : ((values[flop.clear] ?? LZ) as Logic),
});

/** What a flip-flop is driving right now, given the levels on its async pins. */
function flopOutputs(flop: SimFlop, values: Uint8Array, q: Logic) {
  if (flop.power && !isPowered(flop.power, values)) return { q: LZ, qn: LZ };
  return asyncOutputs(asyncOf(flop, values)) ?? heldOutputs(q);
}

/** One combinational settle, with every flip-flop held at its stored value. */
function settle(
  nl: SimNetlist,
  inputs: InputVector,
  options: SimOptions,
  state: SeqState,
): Omit<SimState, "state" | "edges"> {
  const maxCycles = options.maxDeltaCycles ?? DEFAULT_MAX_CYCLES;
  const seed = options.seed ?? LZ;

  let cur = new Uint8Array(nl.netCount);
  let next = new Uint8Array(nl.netCount);

  // Drivers that do not depend on any cell: the rails, plus the switches for
  // this particular input vector.
  const staticDrivers: Driver[][] = nl.railDrivers.map((d) => [...d]);
  nl.inputs.forEach((port, i) => {
    const bit = inputs[i];
    if (bit === undefined) return;
    (staticDrivers[port.net] as Driver[]).push({
      value: bit === 1 ? L1 : L0,
      strength: "strong",
    });
  });
  nl.clocks.forEach((port, i) => {
    const bit = options.clocks?.[i] ?? port.state;
    (staticDrivers[port.net] as Driver[]).push({
      value: bit === 1 ? L1 : L0,
      strength: "strong",
    });
  });

  // A cell's current output, so a net can be resolved from all of its drivers
  // without re-evaluating any of them. THIS is what the seed initializes — see
  // SimOptions.seed. Defaults to Z: a gate that has not been evaluated yet
  // drives nothing.
  const cellOut = new Uint8Array(nl.cells.length).fill(seed);

  const shorted = new Set(nl.railShorts);

  // Which flip-flop outputs land on which net. Built once per settle, because
  // unlike a cell a flip-flop drives TWO nets and the pair is not always
  // complementary — hold preset and clear together and both go high.
  const flopDrivers = driverIndex(nl);
  // A flip-flop's contribution, recomputed each delta cycle from `cur` because
  // its async pins are level-sensitive.
  let flopOut = flopDrive(nl, cur, state);

  const resolve = (net: number, outs: Uint8Array): Logic => {
    // A net shorting Vcc to GND has no honest value. Say X and let diagnostics
    // explain, rather than inventing a level.
    if (shorted.has(net)) return LX;

    const drivers: Driver[] = [...(staticDrivers[net] as Driver[])];
    for (const cellId of cellsDriving(nl, net)) {
      drivers.push({
        value: outs[cellId] as Logic,
        strength: "strong",
      });
    }
    for (const slot of flopDrivers[net] ?? []) {
      drivers.push({ value: flopOut[slot] as Logic, strength: "strong" });
    }
    return resolveNet(drivers);
  };

  // Seed every net from its static drivers so rails and switches are live before
  // the first cell is ever evaluated.
  for (let net = 0; net < nl.netCount; net++) {
    cur[net] = resolve(net, cellOut);
  }
  flopOut = flopDrive(nl, cur, state);

  const seen = new Map<string, number>();
  let settled = false;
  let cycles = 0;
  let period = 0;

  for (cycles = 0; cycles < maxCycles; cycles++) {
    // --- 1. evaluate every cell against `cur` ONLY ------------------------
    flopOut = flopDrive(nl, cur, state);
    const nextCellOut = new Uint8Array(cellOut);
    for (const cell of nl.cells) {
      let value: Logic;

      if (cell.power && !isPowered(cell.power, cur)) {
        // An unpowered chip drives nothing. Not 0 — nothing. Boolean logic
        // cannot express this, which is why we are not using boolean logic.
        value = LZ;
      } else {
        value = evalGate(
          cell.op,
          cell.inputs.map((n) => cur[n] as Logic),
        );
      }

      nextCellOut[cell.id] = value;
    }

    // --- 2. resolve every net from the new cell outputs -------------------
    for (let net = 0; net < nl.netCount; net++) {
      next[net] = resolve(net, nextCellOut);
    }

    // --- 3. settled? -------------------------------------------------------
    // This check MUST come before cycle detection below. A fixed point is itself
    // a period-1 cycle — it repeats its own state forever — so a hash check
    // placed first would flag every stable circuit as an oscillator.
    if (equal(cur, next)) {
      cellOut.set(nextCellOut);
      settled = true;
      break;
    }

    // --- 4. cycle detection ------------------------------------------------
    // Because f is deterministic and the state space is finite, a repeated state
    // means we are in a genuine loop (period >= 2), definitively — not a
    // heuristic. Fixed points were already claimed above.
    const key = hash(next);
    const before = seen.get(key);
    if (before !== undefined) {
      period = cycles - before;
      break;
    }
    seen.set(key, cycles);

    cellOut.set(nextCellOut);
    const swap = cur;
    cur = next;
    next = swap;
  }

  if (settled) {
    return {
      values: cur,
      settled: true,
      deltaCycles: cycles,
      oscillating: [],
      period: 0,
    };
  }

  // --- oscillation ---------------------------------------------------------
  // Do NOT nuke the board. Replay the loop, and only the nets that actually took
  // more than one value inside it become X; everything stable keeps its value.
  // A three-inverter ring should turn ITS OWN loop red, not the whole circuit.
  const { values, oscillating } = collapseOscillation(
    nl,
    cur,
    staticDrivers,
    cellOut,
    shorted,
    Math.max(1, period),
    flopDrivers,
    flopOut,
  );

  return {
    values,
    settled: false,
    deltaCycles: cycles,
    oscillating,
    period,
  };
}

/**
 * Run the loop once more, note which nets are unstable, pin those to X, then let
 * X propagate downstream one final time — honouring controlling values, so an
 * `AND(0, X)` downstream still yields a clean 0.
 */
function collapseOscillation(
  nl: SimNetlist,
  start: Uint8Array,
  staticDrivers: readonly (readonly Driver[])[],
  cellOut: Uint8Array,
  shorted: ReadonlySet<number>,
  period: number,
  flopDrivers: readonly (readonly number[] | undefined)[] = [],
  flopOut: Uint8Array = new Uint8Array(),
): { values: Uint8Array; oscillating: number[] } {
  const resolve = (net: number, outs: Uint8Array): Logic => {
    if (shorted.has(net)) return LX;
    const drivers: Driver[] = [...(staticDrivers[net] as Driver[])];
    for (const cellId of cellsDriving(nl, net)) {
      drivers.push({ value: outs[cellId] as Logic, strength: "strong" });
    }
    for (const slot of flopDrivers[net] ?? []) {
      drivers.push({ value: flopOut[slot] as Logic, strength: "strong" });
    }
    return resolveNet(drivers);
  };

  const seenValues: Set<Logic>[] = Array.from(
    { length: nl.netCount },
    (_, net) => new Set<Logic>([start[net] as Logic]),
  );

  let cur = new Uint8Array(start);
  let outs = new Uint8Array(cellOut);

  for (let step = 0; step < period; step++) {
    const nextOuts = new Uint8Array(outs);
    for (const cell of nl.cells) {
      nextOuts[cell.id] =
        cell.power && !isPowered(cell.power, cur)
          ? LZ
          : evalGate(
              cell.op,
              cell.inputs.map((n) => cur[n] as Logic),
            );
    }

    const nextValues = new Uint8Array(nl.netCount);
    for (let net = 0; net < nl.netCount; net++) {
      nextValues[net] = resolve(net, nextOuts);
      (seenValues[net] as Set<Logic>).add(nextValues[net] as Logic);
    }

    cur = nextValues;
    outs = nextOuts;
  }

  const oscillating: number[] = [];
  const values = new Uint8Array(cur);
  for (let net = 0; net < nl.netCount; net++) {
    if ((seenValues[net] as Set<Logic>).size > 1) {
      oscillating.push(net);
      values[net] = LX;
    }
  }

  // One settling pass so X reaches the gates downstream of the loop.
  for (let pass = 0; pass < 2; pass++) {
    const nextOuts = new Uint8Array(outs);
    for (const cell of nl.cells) {
      nextOuts[cell.id] =
        cell.power && !isPowered(cell.power, values)
          ? LZ
          : evalGate(
              cell.op,
              cell.inputs.map((n) => values[n] as Logic),
            );
    }
    for (let net = 0; net < nl.netCount; net++) {
      if (oscillating.includes(net)) continue; // stays pinned to X
      values[net] = resolve(net, nextOuts);
    }
    outs = nextOuts;
  }

  return { values, oscillating };
}

/** An IC is powered iff its Vcc pin resolves to 1 and its GND pin to 0. */
function isPowered(
  power: { vcc: number; gnd: number },
  values: Uint8Array,
): boolean {
  if (power.vcc < 0 || power.gnd < 0) return false; // pin not on any net at all
  return values[power.vcc] === L1 && values[power.gnd] === L0;
}

/**
 * Net -> the flip-flop output slots landing on it.
 *
 * Two slots per flip-flop, `2i` for Q and `2i+1` for Q-bar, so one flat array
 * indexes both without a second structure. A package with no complementary
 * output simply never registers its odd slot.
 */
function driverIndex(nl: SimNetlist): (number[] | undefined)[] {
  const index: (number[] | undefined)[] = new Array(nl.netCount);
  const put = (net: number, slot: number) => {
    const list = index[net] ?? [];
    list.push(slot);
    index[net] = list;
  };
  for (const flop of nl.flops) {
    put(flop.q, flop.id * 2);
    if (flop.qn !== null) put(flop.qn, flop.id * 2 + 1);
  }
  return index;
}

/** Every flip-flop's two output values, against the current net vector. */
function flopDrive(nl: SimNetlist, values: Uint8Array, state: SeqState): Uint8Array {
  const out = new Uint8Array(nl.flops.length * 2);
  for (const flop of nl.flops) {
    const driven = flopOutputs(flop, values, state.q[flop.id] as Logic);
    out[flop.id * 2] = driven.q;
    out[flop.id * 2 + 1] = driven.qn;
  }
  return out;
}

/**
 * Which cells drive this net. Derived rather than cached — the cell count is
 * small and this keeps SimNetlist free of a redundant index that could drift.
 */
function cellsDriving(nl: SimNetlist, net: number): number[] {
  const out: number[] = [];
  for (const cell of nl.cells) {
    if (cell.output === net) out.push(cell.id);
  }
  return out;
}

const equal = (a: Uint8Array, b: Uint8Array): boolean => {
  for (let i = 0; i < a.length; i++) if (a[i] !== b[i]) return false;
  return true;
};

/** FNV-1a over the value vector. Only needs to be a good map key. */
function hash(values: Uint8Array): string {
  let h = 0x811c9dc5;
  for (let i = 0; i < values.length; i++) {
    h ^= values[i] as number;
    h = Math.imul(h, 0x01000193);
  }
  return (h >>> 0).toString(36);
}

export { LX, LZ, L0, L1 };


=== FILE: src/lib/simulation/timing.ts ===
import type { SimNetlist } from "./elaborate";
import { evaluate, initialState, type SeqState } from "./solver";
import { evalGate, resolveNet, L0, L1, LZ, type Driver, type Logic } from "./logic";

/**
 * The unit-delay timed solver, and the counter-driven waveform on top of it.
 *
 * WHY THIS EXISTS, given that a timing diagram over combinational logic driven by
 * manual switch flips would be nearly content-free:
 *
 * 1. COUNTER-DRIVEN INPUTS. A toggles every 2 ticks, B every 4, C every 8. The
 *    waveform then sweeps all 2^n input combinations over time — it becomes a
 *    logic-analyzer view of the truth table, which is worth looking at.
 *
 * 2. UNIT DELAY. The delta-cycle solver in solver.ts iterates to a fixpoint and
 *    reports only the settled answer. It cannot show a glitch, because a glitch
 *    is a transient that the fixpoint has already swallowed. Here, each gate
 *    takes exactly one tick to respond, so signals arriving by different-length
 *    paths arrive at different times — and STATIC HAZARDS become visible as
 *    momentary spikes on the output.
 *
 * And that is the payoff, because it closes the loop back to the theory module.
 * A minimal SOP can glitch. Adding back the redundant consensus prime implicant —
 * the one Quine-McCluskey discarded precisely BECAUSE it was redundant — removes
 * the glitch. Minimal is not the same as hazard-free, and now you can see it.
 */

export interface TimedOptions {
  /** Gate propagation delay, in ticks. */
  readonly delay?: number;
  /** Ticks to settle before the first input change, so t=0 is not a fake edge. */
  readonly settleTicks?: number;
  /** Clock cycles to run, for a clocked circuit. Ignored by the input sweep. */
  readonly cycles?: number;
  /**
   * Where the flip-flops are NOW.
   *
   * The chart answers "what happens over the next few cycles", and that
   * question has no answer independent of where the circuit currently is. Left
   * out, it starts from power-up — every stored bit unknown — which is exactly
   * what an uncleared counter really does, and exactly what the chart should
   * show when that is the truth.
   */
  readonly state?: SeqState;
}

export interface Waveform {
  readonly label: string;
  readonly net: number;
  /** One sample per tick. */
  readonly samples: readonly Logic[];
  readonly isInput: boolean;
}

export interface TimedResult {
  readonly ticks: number;
  readonly waves: readonly Waveform[];
  /**
   * Ticks at which an output pulsed away from its settled value and back — a
   * glitch. Reported per output waveform.
   */
  readonly glitches: readonly { readonly label: string; readonly ticks: readonly number[] }[];
  /** Ticks per held input combination, so the UI can draw the step markers. */
  readonly ticksPerCombination: number;
  /** Total held steps. The sweep runs forward and then back — see simulateTimed. */
  readonly steps: number;
  /** Tick at which the sweep begins (everything before it is the settling period). */
  readonly settleTicks: number;
  /** The input combination held during each step, for aligning the UI. */
  readonly combinations: readonly number[];
  /**
   * Set when the circuit has too many inputs to sweep, instead of a waveform.
   *
   * A COMPLETE sweep or none, and never a partial one presented as a whole.
   * The two things this chart claims — "here is every input combination" and
   * "no output glitches" — are both claims about the WHOLE input space, and a
   * chart drawn from the first 128 combinations of 131,072 would be making both
   * of them falsely.
   */
  readonly tooWide?: { readonly inputs: number; readonly combinations: number };
  /**
   * Set when the chart was driven by the CLOCK rather than by an input sweep.
   *
   * The two charts answer different questions and must not be confused for one
   * another. A sweep says "here is the output for every input combination" —
   * a statement about a function. A clocked chart says "here is what happened
   * over these clock cycles" — a statement about a sequence, which is the only
   * thing a circuit with memory has.
   */
  readonly clocked?: { readonly cycles: number };
}

const DEFAULT_DELAY = 1;

/**
 * The most inputs this will sweep. 2^7 combinations, forward and back, is 256
 * held steps — already a chart you have to scroll.
 *
 * There was no limit at all, and every term of the cost is exponential: the
 * 4-bit ripple adder's 9 inputs produced an 11,275-tick chart 79,000 pixels
 * wide, which had no chance of being read and — because a wide child of a CSS
 * grid track widens the track — pushed the schematic, the fault panel and half
 * the page off the side of the window. Its 8-bit sibling asked for 262,144
 * steps and never returned; the 16-bit one asked for 17 billion and threw.
 *
 * The presets that ask for those are legitimate circuits. It is the SWEEP that
 * does not scale, so the sweep is what says no.
 */
export const MAX_SWEEP_INPUTS = 7;

/**
 * Reflected binary (Gray) code. Successive values differ in exactly ONE bit.
 *
 * The same function the K-map uses, and here for the same underlying reason.
 */
const gray = (i: number): number => i ^ (i >>> 1);

/**
 * Drive the inputs from a GRAY-CODE counter and record every net over time.
 *
 * Gray code, not plain binary, and this is load-bearing rather than cosmetic.
 *
 * A binary counter steps 011 -> 100, changing all three inputs at once. Any
 * output transient across a multi-input change is a FUNCTION HAZARD, and function
 * hazards are unavoidable — no amount of redundant logic can remove them, because
 * the function itself is doing something different at the two endpoints. Sweeping
 * in binary therefore produces glitches that look like the ones we want to teach
 * about but cannot be cured, which would make the lesson actively misleading.
 *
 * A Gray counter changes exactly one input per step. The only transients left are
 * LOGIC HAZARDS — caused by unequal path delays inside the gate network, not by
 * the function — and those are exactly the ones a redundant consensus term
 * removes. That is the lesson, and it only exists in Gray order.
 *
 * AND THE SWEEP RUNS FORWARD, THEN BACK. A Gray sequence visits each combination
 * once, so it crosses each adjacent pair in only ONE direction — and a hazard is
 * directional. The classic static-1 in `A·C' + B·C` appears when C FALLS with
 * A=B=1 (the B·C term collapses immediately while A·C' is still waiting on the
 * inverter), and not when C rises. Sweep one way only and you would never see it.
 * Running the palindrome exercises every adjacent transition in both directions,
 * which is exactly what you would do on a bench to hunt for glitches.
 */
export function simulateTimed(
  nl: SimNetlist,
  options: TimedOptions = {},
): TimedResult {
  const delay = Math.max(1, options.delay ?? DEFAULT_DELAY);
  const n = nl.inputs.length;

  const nothing: TimedResult = {
    ticks: 0,
    waves: [],
    glitches: [],
    ticksPerCombination: 0,
    steps: 0,
    settleTicks: 0,
    combinations: [],
  };

  // A circuit with memory has no truth table to sweep, so the clock drives the
  // chart instead. This is not a fallback — it is the right chart for the
  // circuit, and the one a counter is always drawn with.
  if (nl.flops.length > 0) return simulateClocked(nl, options);

  if (n === 0 || nl.cells.length === 0) return nothing;

  // Refuse before allocating anything. `2 ** 33` steps is not a slow sweep, it
  // is an `Array.from` that throws — and the failure landed as an empty lab
  // with no explanation of what had happened to the circuit.
  if (n > MAX_SWEEP_INPUTS) {
    return { ...nothing, tooWide: { inputs: n, combinations: 2 ** n } };
  }

  // Each input combination must be held long enough for the deepest path to
  // propagate AND for any glitch to be visible before we move on.
  const depth = longestPath(nl);
  const ticksPerCombination = Math.max(4, (depth + 2) * delay);
  const settle = options.settleTicks ?? ticksPerCombination;

  // Forward through the Gray sequence, then back — see the note above.
  const sweep = 1 << n;
  const steps = 2 * sweep;
  const combinations = Array.from({ length: steps }, (_, s) =>
    gray(s < sweep ? s : steps - 1 - s),
  );
  const ticks = settle + steps * ticksPerCombination;

  const values = new Uint8Array(nl.netCount);
  /** Each cell's output, and the tick at which its currently-pending value lands. */
  const cellOut = new Uint8Array(nl.cells.length).fill(LZ);
  const pending = new Uint8Array(nl.cells.length).fill(LZ);
  const pendingAt = new Int32Array(nl.cells.length).fill(-1);

  const shorted = new Set(nl.railShorts);
  const samples: Logic[][] = Array.from({ length: nl.netCount }, () => []);

  for (let t = 0; t < ticks; t++) {
    // --- 1. the Gray-code input counter, forward then back -----------------
    const phase = Math.max(0, t - settle);
    const stepIndex = Math.min(
      steps - 1,
      Math.floor(phase / ticksPerCombination),
    );
    const combination = combinations[stepIndex] as number;

    const staticDrivers: Driver[][] = nl.railDrivers.map((d) => [...d]);
    nl.inputs.forEach((port, i) => {
      const bit = ((combination >>> (n - 1 - i)) & 1) as 0 | 1;
      (staticDrivers[port.net] as Driver[]).push({
        value: bit === 1 ? L1 : L0,
        strength: "strong",
      });
    });

    // --- 2. resolve nets from the CURRENT cell outputs ---------------------
    for (let net = 0; net < nl.netCount; net++) {
      if (shorted.has(net)) {
        values[net] = 3;
        continue;
      }
      const drivers: Driver[] = [...(staticDrivers[net] as Driver[])];
      for (const cell of nl.cells) {
        if (cell.output === net) {
          drivers.push({ value: cellOut[cell.id] as Logic, strength: "strong" });
        }
      }
      values[net] = resolveNet(drivers);
    }

    for (let net = 0; net < nl.netCount; net++) {
      (samples[net] as Logic[]).push(values[net] as Logic);
    }

    // --- 3. schedule each gate's response, `delay` ticks from now ----------
    //
    // THIS is what makes a glitch possible. In the fixpoint solver every gate
    // updates in the same instant, so a signal that races down a short path and
    // one that takes a long path arrive together and cancel. Here they don't:
    // the short path lands first, the output moves, and only later does the long
    // path arrive and put it back. That momentary spike is the static hazard.
    for (const cell of nl.cells) {
      const want =
        cell.power && !isPowered(cell.power, values)
          ? LZ
          : evalGate(
              cell.op,
              cell.inputs.map((net) => values[net] as Logic),
            );

      if (want !== (pending[cell.id] as Logic)) {
        pending[cell.id] = want;
        pendingAt[cell.id] = t + delay;
      }
    }

    // --- 4. land the responses that are due --------------------------------
    for (const cell of nl.cells) {
      if (pendingAt[cell.id] === t + 1) {
        cellOut[cell.id] = pending[cell.id] as Logic;
      }
    }
  }

  const inputNets = new Set(nl.inputs.map((p) => p.net));
  const waves: Waveform[] = [];

  for (const port of nl.inputs) {
    waves.push({
      label: port.label,
      net: port.net,
      samples: samples[port.net] as Logic[],
      isInput: true,
    });
  }
  for (const port of nl.outputs) {
    if (inputNets.has(port.net)) continue;
    waves.push({
      label: port.label,
      net: port.net,
      samples: samples[port.net] as Logic[],
      isInput: false,
    });
  }

  return {
    ticks,
    waves,
    glitches: waves
      .filter((w) => !w.isInput)
      .map((w) => ({
        label: w.label,
        ticks: findGlitches(w.samples, settle, ticksPerCombination),
      }))
      .filter((g) => g.ticks.length > 0),
    ticksPerCombination,
    steps,
    settleTicks: settle,
    combinations,
  };
}

/**
 * A glitch is a transient: within ONE held input combination, the signal moves
 * away from the value it eventually settles on, and then comes back.
 *
 * We compare each sample against the value at the END of its own window — which
 * is by construction the settled value, since the window is longer than the
 * longest path.
 */
function findGlitches(
  samples: readonly Logic[],
  settle: number,
  windowSize: number,
): number[] {
  const out: number[] = [];

  for (let start = settle; start + windowSize <= samples.length; start += windowSize) {
    const settled = samples[start + windowSize - 1] as Logic;

    // Skip the leading edge — the output is *allowed* to be in transit right
    // after the inputs change. A glitch is a spike AFTER it first arrives.
    let arrived = -1;
    for (let t = start; t < start + windowSize; t++) {
      if (samples[t] === settled) {
        arrived = t;
        break;
      }
    }
    if (arrived < 0) continue;

    for (let t = arrived; t < start + windowSize; t++) {
      if (samples[t] !== settled) out.push(t);
    }
  }

  return out;
}

/** Longest combinational path, in gates. Sets how long each input must be held. */
function longestPath(nl: SimNetlist): number {
  const depth = new Int32Array(nl.netCount);
  // The cells are in construction order, which is close enough to topological
  // for a bound; iterate a few times so feedback does not under-report.
  for (let pass = 0; pass < Math.min(8, nl.cells.length + 1); pass++) {
    for (const cell of nl.cells) {
      const d = Math.max(0, ...cell.inputs.map((net) => depth[net] as number)) + 1;
      if (d > (depth[cell.output] as number)) depth[cell.output] = d;
    }
  }
  return Math.max(1, ...depth);
}

function isPowered(power: { vcc: number; gnd: number }, values: Uint8Array): boolean {
  if (power.vcc < 0 || power.gnd < 0) return false;
  return values[power.vcc] === L1 && values[power.gnd] === L0;
}


// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Clocked circuits
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/** How many clock cycles a clocked chart runs for. Enough to see a 4-bit count. */
const DEFAULT_CYCLES = 18;
/** Ticks per half cycle. Only affects how wide the drawn pulses are. */
const HALF = 3;

/**
 * The waveform of a clocked circuit: drive the clock, watch the state.
 *
 * SAMPLED SETTLED, ONCE PER TICK. The combinational chart above models a unit
 * gate delay explicitly, because its whole purpose is to show a glitch — a
 * transient that only exists between gate delays. This one has a different
 * purpose and makes a different, weaker claim: each sample is the circuit after
 * it has finished reacting. A ripple counter's stages really do settle at
 * different moments and this chart does not show that, so it does not say it
 * does; what it shows is the count, which is what a counter's timing diagram is
 * for.
 *
 * The switches are held at whatever the document has them at. Sweeping them
 * would be sweeping inputs of a circuit whose output is not a function of its
 * inputs.
 */
function simulateClocked(nl: SimNetlist, options: TimedOptions): TimedResult {
  const cycles = options.cycles ?? DEFAULT_CYCLES;
  const ticks = cycles * HALF * 2;

  // Held where the user left them. Zeroing them would assert every active-low
  // clear on the board, and a counter that is permanently cleared counts zero.
  const inputs = nl.inputs.map((port) => port.state ?? 0);
  const samples: Logic[][] = Array.from({ length: nl.netCount }, () => []);

  // Start from power-up and let the first tick establish the clock's resting
  // level, exactly as the app does when a circuit is loaded.
  let state = options.state ?? initialState(nl);

  for (let t = 0; t < ticks; t++) {
    const high = Math.floor(t / HALF) % 2 === 1;
    const result = evaluate(nl, inputs, {
      state,
      clocks: nl.clocks.map(() => (high ? 1 : 0)),
    });
    state = result.state;
    for (let net = 0; net < nl.netCount; net++) {
      (samples[net] as Logic[]).push(result.values[net] as Logic);
    }
  }

  const waves: Waveform[] = [
    ...nl.clocks.map((port) => ({
      label: port.label,
      net: port.net,
      samples: samples[port.net] as Logic[],
      isInput: true,
    })),
    ...nl.inputs.map((port) => ({
      label: port.label,
      net: port.net,
      samples: samples[port.net] as Logic[],
      isInput: true,
    })),
    ...nl.outputs.map((port) => ({
      label: port.label,
      net: port.net,
      samples: samples[port.net] as Logic[],
      isInput: false,
    })),
  ];

  return {
    ticks,
    waves,
    // A glitch is a transient between gate delays, and this chart does not
    // model gate delays. Reporting an empty list would be a claim; reporting
    // nothing at all is the honest shape.
    glitches: [],
    ticksPerCombination: HALF * 2,
    steps: cycles,
    settleTicks: 0,
    combinations: [],
    clocked: { cycles },
  };
}


=== FILE: src/lib/simulation/ic-library.ts ===
import type { GateOp } from "./logic";
import type { PinSpec } from "./netlist";
import { DATA_PINS, type Edge, type FlopKind } from "./sequential";
import {
  registerIcLibrary,
  type CellTemplate,
  type FlopTemplate,
  type IcRegistry,
  type PowerPins,
} from "./parts";

/**
 * The 74xx TTL library.
 *
 * THE DESIGN RULE THAT MAKES THIS CORRECT: transcribe the datasheet's PIN NAMES,
 * and DERIVE the gate map from them. Never hand-write the gate map.
 *
 * Why it matters: the parts are NOT all shaped alike, and it is very easy to
 * assume they are.
 *
 *   7400 pin 1 = 1A, pin 2 = 1B, pin 3 = 1Y     (inputs first)
 *   7402 pin 1 = 1Y, pin 2 = 1A, pin 3 = 1B     (OUTPUT first!)
 *
 * And both of them flip layout again on the right-hand half of the package.
 * Pattern-matching one chip's shape onto another produces a chip that simulates
 * beautifully and is wired to the wrong pins on a real breadboard — the worst
 * possible failure for a lab assistant.
 *
 * So the only thing a human writes here is fourteen strings copied off a
 * datasheet. `parsePinNames` turns "1A"/"1Y" into gate slots, and
 * `validateDefinition` refuses anything inconsistent. Adding a new chip is one
 * line, and there is nowhere to put a typo that a test will not catch.
 */

export interface IcDefinition {
  readonly part: string;
  readonly name: string;
  readonly pinNames: readonly string[];
  /** Meaningless for a sequential part; `gateCount` is 0 there. */
  readonly op: GateOp;
  readonly pins: readonly PinSpec[];
  readonly cells: readonly CellTemplate[];
  /** Empty for a combinational part. */
  readonly flops: readonly FlopTemplate[];
  readonly power: PowerPins;
  readonly gateCount: number;
  readonly inputsPerGate: number;
  /** One line for the palette, so a chooser can say what the part is for. */
  readonly summary?: string;
}

/** Does this package contain state? Decides which palette section it lands in. */
export const isSequential = (def: IcDefinition): boolean => def.flops.length > 0;

// --- DIP-14 geometry --------------------------------------------------------

/** One hole = 0.1 inch. At 24px/hole a DIP14 is 144x72 on the canvas. */
export const PIN_PITCH = 24;
/** 0.3 inch across the notch — a real DIP straddles the breadboard channel. */
export const ROW_SPAN = 3 * PIN_PITCH;
export const DIP_INSET = PIN_PITCH / 2;

export const dipWidth = (pinCount: number): number =>
  (pinCount / 2 - 1) * PIN_PITCH + PIN_PITCH;
export const dipHeight = (): number => ROW_SPAN;

/**
 * DIP pin numbering is counter-clockwise from the notch: pin 1 is bottom-left,
 * pins 1..7 run left-to-right along the bottom, then 8..14 run RIGHT-TO-LEFT
 * along the top. So pin 8 sits directly across from pin 7, and pin 14 across
 * from pin 1 — which is exactly why Vcc (14) and GND (7) end up at opposite
 * corners.
 */
export function pinOffset(pin: number, pinCount: number): { x: number; y: number } {
  const perRow = pinCount / 2;
  if (pin <= perRow) {
    return { x: DIP_INSET + (pin - 1) * PIN_PITCH, y: ROW_SPAN };
  }
  return { x: DIP_INSET + (pinCount - pin) * PIN_PITCH, y: 0 };
}

// --- the parser -------------------------------------------------------------

const GATE_INPUT = /^(\d+)([A-H])$/; // 1A, 2B … up to 1H on the 8-input 7430
const GATE_OUTPUT = /^(\d+)Y$/; // 1Y, 2Y…

/**
 * Turn the datasheet pin-name array into pins, cells, and power pins.
 *
 * This is the whole trick: the gate map is *computed*, so it cannot disagree
 * with the pinout it was supposed to describe.
 */
function defineDip(
  part: string,
  name: string,
  op: GateOp,
  pinNames: readonly string[],
): IcDefinition {
  const pinCount = pinNames.length;

  const pins: PinSpec[] = pinNames.map((pinName, i) => {
    const number = i + 1;
    const offset = pinOffset(number, pinCount);

    if (pinName === "VCC") {
      return { number, name: pinName, dir: "pwr", strength: "supply", offset };
    }
    if (pinName === "GND") {
      return { number, name: pinName, dir: "gnd", strength: "supply", offset };
    }
    if (pinName === "NC") {
      return { number, name: pinName, dir: "nc", strength: "hiz", offset };
    }
    if (GATE_OUTPUT.test(pinName)) {
      return { number, name: pinName, dir: "out", strength: "strong", offset };
    }
    if (GATE_INPUT.test(pinName)) {
      return { number, name: pinName, dir: "in", strength: "hiz", offset };
    }
    throw new Error(`${part}: unrecognized pin name "${pinName}"`);
  });

  // Group by gate index. The input letters give the ordering, so a chip whose
  // inputs are physically out of order still gets its gate assembled correctly.
  const slots = new Map<number, { inputs: string[]; output: string | null }>();
  for (const pinName of pinNames) {
    const out = GATE_OUTPUT.exec(pinName);
    const inp = GATE_INPUT.exec(pinName);
    const slot = out ? Number(out[1]) : inp ? Number(inp[1]) : null;
    if (slot === null) continue;

    const entry = slots.get(slot) ?? { inputs: [], output: null };
    if (out) entry.output = pinName;
    else entry.inputs.push(pinName);
    slots.set(slot, entry);
  }

  const cells: CellTemplate[] = [...slots.entries()]
    .sort(([a], [b]) => a - b)
    .map(([slot, entry]) => {
      if (!entry.output) throw new Error(`${part}: gate ${slot} has no Y pin`);
      return {
        slot,
        op,
        inputPins: [...entry.inputs].sort(), // 1A before 1B before 1C
        outputPin: entry.output,
      };
    });

  const def: IcDefinition = {
    part,
    name,
    pinNames,
    op,
    pins,
    cells,
    flops: [],
    power: { vcc: "VCC", gnd: "GND" },
    gateCount: cells.length,
    inputsPerGate: cells[0]?.inputPins.length ?? 0,
  };

  const problems = validateDefinition(def);
  if (problems.length > 0) {
    throw new Error(`${part}: ${problems.join("; ")}`);
  }
  return def;
}

export function validateDefinition(def: IcDefinition): string[] {
  const problems: string[] = [];

  if (def.pinNames.length % 2 !== 0) problems.push("odd pin count");
  if (!def.pinNames.includes("VCC")) problems.push("no VCC pin");
  if (!def.pinNames.includes("GND")) problems.push("no GND pin");

  const seen = new Set<string>();
  for (const name of def.pinNames) {
    if (name !== "NC" && seen.has(name)) problems.push(`duplicate pin ${name}`);
    seen.add(name);
  }

  for (const cell of def.cells) {
    if (cell.inputPins.length === 0) problems.push(`gate ${cell.slot} has no inputs`);
    if (cell.inputPins.length !== def.inputsPerGate) {
      problems.push(`gate ${cell.slot} has inconsistent arity`);
    }
  }

  return problems;
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// The parts. Fourteen strings each, copied off the datasheet, pin 1 -> pin 14.
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

export const IC_7400 = defineDip("7400", "Quad 2-input NAND", "nand", [
  "1A", "1B", "1Y", "2A", "2B", "2Y", "GND",   // pins 1-7
  "3Y", "3A", "3B", "4Y", "4A", "4B", "VCC",   // pins 8-14
]);

/**
 * THE ONE EVERYONE GETS WRONG.
 *
 * The 7402 is NOT shaped like the 7400. Its left half puts the OUTPUT FIRST —
 * gate 1 is inputs (2,3) -> output 1 — and then its right half flips back to
 * the A,B,Y order. Transcribe it; do not pattern-match it.
 */
export const IC_7402 = defineDip("7402", "Quad 2-input NOR", "nor", [
  "1Y", "1A", "1B", "2Y", "2A", "2B", "GND",   // pins 1-7   (Y FIRST)
  "3A", "3B", "3Y", "4A", "4B", "4Y", "VCC",   // pins 8-14  (Y last)
]);

/** The 7404 also reverses on the right half: pin 8 is 4Y, pin 9 is 4A. */
export const IC_7404 = defineDip("7404", "Hex inverter", "not", [
  "1A", "1Y", "2A", "2Y", "3A", "3Y", "GND",   // pins 1-7

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Sequential parts
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/**
 * A flip-flop package, derived from its pin names exactly as the gates are.
 *
 * SAME RULE, AND IT MATTERS MORE HERE. The only thing written by hand is the
 * datasheet's list of pin names; the flip-flop map is computed from it. These
 * parts are far less alike than the gates are — the '76 puts VCC on pin 5 and
 * GND on pin 13 rather than in the usual corners, the '107 has a clear and no
 * preset, and the '175 shares one clock and one clear across four flip-flops —
 * so pattern-matching one onto another produces a chip that simulates
 * beautifully and is wired to the wrong pins on a real board.
 *
 * The grammar is the silkscreen's own: a leading digit is the flip-flop number,
 * and a name with NO digit is shared by every flip-flop in the package. That
 * one rule is what makes a '74 (two independent clocks) and a '175 (one common
 * clock) the same nine lines of code.
 */
const SEQ_PIN = /^(\d*)(CLK|CLR|PRE|QN|D|T|J|K|S|R|Q)$/;

type Role = "CLK" | "CLR" | "PRE" | "QN" | "D" | "T" | "J" | "K" | "S" | "R" | "Q";

const OUTPUT_ROLES: readonly Role[] = ["Q", "QN"];

function defineSeqDip(
  part: string,
  name: string,
  kind: FlopKind,
  edge: Edge,
  summary: string,
  pinNames: readonly string[],
): IcDefinition {
  const pinCount = pinNames.length;

  const roleOf = (pinName: string): { slot: number | null; role: Role } | null => {
    const m = SEQ_PIN.exec(pinName);
    if (!m) return null;
    return { slot: m[1] ? Number(m[1]) : null, role: m[2] as Role };
  };

  const pins: PinSpec[] = pinNames.map((pinName, i) => {
    const number = i + 1;
    const offset = pinOffset(number, pinCount);

    if (pinName === "VCC") {
      return { number, name: pinName, dir: "pwr", strength: "supply", offset };
    }
    if (pinName === "GND") {
      return { number, name: pinName, dir: "gnd", strength: "supply", offset };
    }
    if (pinName === "NC") {
      return { number, name: pinName, dir: "nc", strength: "hiz", offset };
    }
    const parsed = roleOf(pinName);
    if (!parsed) throw new Error(`${part}: unrecognized pin name "${pinName}"`);
    const out = OUTPUT_ROLES.includes(parsed.role);
    return {
      number,
      name: pinName,
      dir: out ? "out" : "in",
      strength: out ? "strong" : "hiz",
      offset,
    };
  });

  // Numbered names belong to one flip-flop; unnumbered ones to all of them.
  const perSlot = new Map<number, Map<Role, string>>();
  const shared = new Map<Role, string>();
  for (const pinName of pinNames) {
    const parsed = roleOf(pinName);
    if (!parsed) continue;
    if (parsed.slot === null) {
      shared.set(parsed.role, pinName);
      continue;
    }
    const entry = perSlot.get(parsed.slot) ?? new Map<Role, string>();
    entry.set(parsed.role, pinName);
    perSlot.set(parsed.slot, entry);
  }

  const dataRoles = DATA_PINS[kind] as readonly Role[];

  const flops: FlopTemplate[] = [...perSlot.entries()]
    .sort(([a], [b]) => a - b)
    .map(([slot, own]) => {
      const pin = (role: Role): string | null => own.get(role) ?? shared.get(role) ?? null;
      const need = (role: Role): string => {
        const found = pin(role);
        if (!found) throw new Error(`${part}: flip-flop ${slot} has no ${role} pin`);
        return found;
      };
      return {
        slot,
        kind,
        edge,
        clockPin: need("CLK"),
        dataPins: dataRoles.map(need),
        presetPin: pin("PRE"),
        clearPin: pin("CLR"),
        qPin: need("Q"),
        qnPin: pin("QN"),
      };
    });

  if (flops.length === 0) throw new Error(`${part}: no flip-flops found in the pin names`);

  const def: IcDefinition = {
    part,
    name,
    summary,
    pinNames,
    op: "buf", // unused; a sequential package has no gate op
    pins,
    cells: [],
    flops,
    power: { vcc: "VCC", gnd: "GND" },
    gateCount: 0,
    inputsPerGate: 0,
  };

  const problems = validateDefinition(def);
  if (problems.length > 0) throw new Error(`${part}: ${problems.join("; ")}`);
  return def;
}

/**
 * SN7474 — dual D-type positive-edge-triggered flip-flops with preset and clear.
 * Pin names from TI SDLS119 (SN5474/SN7474/SN74LS74A), N package, top view.
 */
export const IC_7474 = defineSeqDip(
  "7474",
  "Dual D flip-flop",
  "d",
  "rising",
  "Positive edge. Async preset and clear, both active low.",
  [
    "1CLR", "1D", "1CLK", "1PRE", "1Q", "1QN", "GND",   // pins 1-7
    "2QN", "2Q", "2PRE", "2CLK", "2D", "2CLR", "VCC",   // pins 8-14
  ],
);

/**
 * SN74LS76A — dual J-K flip-flops with preset and clear, NEGATIVE edge.
 * Pin names from TI SDLS121, 16-pin J/N package, top view.
 *
 * TWO THINGS THIS PART DOES NOT SHARE WITH ANY OTHER CHIP HERE. Its supply pins
 * are 5 and 13, not the corners — wire it like a '74 and it does not power up.
 * And the plain '76 is pulse-triggered (master-slave, with the ones-catching
 * that implies), while the 'LS76A is negative-edge-triggered; they share a
 * pinout and not a function table. The edge-triggered part is the one modelled,
 * so the edge-triggered part is the one named.
 */

  "jk",

    "1CLK", "1PRE", "1CLR", "1J", "VCC", "2CLK", "2PRE", "2CLR",   // pins 1-8
    "2J", "2QN", "2Q", "2K", "GND", "1Q", "1QN", "1K",             // pins 9-16
  ],
);

    "2J", "2CLK", "2CLR", "2K", "1CLK", "1CLR", "VCC", // pins 8-14
  ],
);

/**

    "CLK", "3Q", "3QN", "3D", "4D", "4QN", "4Q", "VCC",  // pins 9-16

  [
    "CLR", "1Q", "1D", "2D", "2Q", "3D", "3Q", "GND",   // pins 1-8
    "CLK", "4Q", "4D", "5D", "5Q", "6D", "6Q", "VCC",   // pins 9-16

export const IC_LIBRARY: readonly IcDefinition[] = [
  // 2-input workhorses
  IC_7400,
  IC_7402,
  IC_7404,
  IC_7408,
  IC_7432,
  IC_7486,
  // wider fan-in
  IC_7410,
  IC_7411,
  IC_7420,
  IC_7421,
  IC_7427,
  IC_7430,
  // sequential
  IC_7474,
  IC_74LS76A,
  IC_74LS107A,
  IC_74175,
  IC_74174,
];

/** The packages with state in them, for the palette's own section. */
export const SEQUENTIAL_LIBRARY: readonly IcDefinition[] =
  IC_LIBRARY.filter(isSequential);

/** The plain gate packages. */
export const COMBINATIONAL_LIBRARY: readonly IcDefinition[] =
  IC_LIBRARY.filter((d) => !isSequential(d));

const BY_PART = new Map(IC_LIBRARY.map((d) => [d.part, d]));

export const getIc = (part: string): IcDefinition | undefined => BY_PART.get(part);

/** The chips that implement a given gate op — used by the M5 technology mapper. */
export const icForOp = (op: GateOp): IcDefinition | undefined =>
  IC_LIBRARY.find((d) => !isSequential(d) && d.op === op && d.inputsPerGate <= 2);

const registry: IcRegistry = {
  pins: (part) => must(part).pins,
  cells: (part) => must(part).cells,
  flops: (part) => must(part).flops,
  power: (part) => must(part).power,
  has: (part) => BY_PART.has(part),
};

function must(part: string): IcDefinition {
  const def = BY_PART.get(part);
  if (!def) throw new Error(`Unknown IC part "${part}"`);
  return def;
}

registerIcLibrary(registry);


=== FILE: src/lib/simulation/elaborate.ts ===
import type { Driver, GateOp } from "./logic";
import { L0, L1 } from "./logic";
import { buildNetIndex, netOf, type CircuitDocument, type NetIndex, type NodeId } from "./netlist";
